Digidesign Mbox 1 — Stock Firmware

Complete reverse-engineering reference for revisions 20 and 22 of the Digidesign Mbox 1 firmware, running on a Texas Instruments TAS1020B (8051-core USB audio streaming controller). Every byte of both 8174-byte images is accounted for and classified.

DeviceDigidesign Mbox 1 (USB audio interface, 2000–2001)
ControllerTI TAS1020B — enhanced 8051 core, USB 1.1 full speed, I²S codec port, 2 DMA channels
Image size8174 bytes (0x1FEE) each, loaded at CODE 0x0000 from I²C EEPROM
Executioncopied by the boot ROM into shadow program RAM; MEMCFG.SDW = 1
ToolchainKeil C51 (identified from the startup stub, ?C_INITSEG table and the ?C?CASE jump-table helper in Rev 20)
USB identityVID 0x0DBA, PID 0x1000; bcdDevice 0x0020 (Rev 20) / 0x0022 (Rev 22)

1. Method, and how to read this

Both images were disassembled by recursive traversal from the reset vector and the interrupt table, following every call, jump, conditional branch and jump-table edge. Alignment is therefore guaranteed by construction rather than assumed — a linear sweep of 8051 code drifts silently whenever it starts mid-instruction, and an earlier linear listing of this same image did exactly that.

Coverage below is measured: every byte is marked as belonging to a decoded instruction, to an identified data block, or to erase fill. The unaccounted count is zero for both images.

Reading trap: the same number is two different addresses

8051 bit instructions (JB, JNB, SETB, CLR, MOV C,) take a bit address; byte instructions (MOV, ORL, INC) take a direct byte address. They print identically. Bit address B lives in IRAM byte 0x20 + (B >> 3), bit B & 7.

This firmware uses both meanings of the same literal constantly. In cmd7: JNB 0x2c tests IRAM 0x25 bit 4 (the S/PDIF-selected flag), while three instructions later MOV 0x2c,#0x23 writes IRAM byte 0x2C (a staged chip-register number). Likewise MOV 0x0a,#0x01 queues event 1, but CLR 0x0a clears IRAM 0x21 bit 2. Misreading one for the other produces confident nonsense.

3. Boot and initialisation

The TAS1020B has no internal flash. Its mask boot ROM enumerates first, reads the external I²C EEPROM, validates a header and signature, copies the firmware image into shadow program RAM, sets MEMCFG.SDW so code fetches come from that RAM, and jumps to 0x0000. Everything documented here begins at that jump.

Two details matter for anyone writing replacement firmware. First, USBCTL bit 7 (CONN, the D+ pull-up) is asserted last, only after the codec port and DMA are configured — so a hang anywhere earlier leaves the device invisible on the bus rather than enumerated-but-broken. Second, GLOBCTL bit 0 is CPTEN, the codec port enable, not a USB enable; the codec configuration registers are only writable while it is clear.

4. Main loop and the event queue

The firmware is a single-threaded foreground loop plus interrupt handlers. USB protocol work happens in the INT0 handler; everything slow or stateful is deferred to a one-slot event queue — IRAM byte 0x0A — drained by the foreground loop.

forever:
    if (tick_flag)            # bit 0x20, set by the Timer 0 ISR
        scan_p3_buttons()     # debounce + edge detect on the front panel
        if (panel_changed)
            shift_out_8()     # IRAM 0x22 -> mux/LED chain A
            shift_out_16()    # IRAM 0x23,0x25 -> chain B
        handle_alt_setting_edges()
        tick_flag = 0
    else if (event_code != 0) # IRAM 0x0A
        dispatch_event()      # 14-entry LJMP table

Timer 0 reloads TH0 = 0xCE and sets the tick flag, giving the panel scan a steady period; the ISR also re-enables EA before returning, so USB interrupts are not blocked by panel work.

4.1 The 14 deferred events

A handler queues an event by writing its number to IRAM 0x0A. The dispatcher subtracts 1, range-checks against 14, multiplies by 3 and jumps into a table of LJMPs. Rev 20 keeps that table in a separate function at 0x0300; Rev 22 inlines it at 0x030C and uses MUL AB instead of two adds.

EventMeaningRev 20Rev 22What it does
1apply clock mode0x032A0x0336Tear down DMA + CPTEN, set CPTCNF3 byte order per direction, re-run the audio path.
2interface 1 alt changed0x03860x038ACapture path. Arms IEPCNF1 = 0xC5 and DMACTL1 |= DMAEN, or disarms them.
3interface 2 alt changed0x03FD0x03FDPlayback path. Arms OEPCNF2 = 0xC5 and DMACTL0 |= DMAEN, or disarms them.
4select analog input0x04540x045AClears the S/PDIF flag (bit 0x2C), commits both shift registers, reapplies clock mode.
5select S/PDIF input0x04660x0469Sets bit 0x2C, forces clock mode 1.
6set clock mode 10x04780x0478Idle / no sample clock.
7set clock mode 2 + program S/PDIF chip0x04800x047D44.1 kHz.
8set clock mode 3 + program S/PDIF chip0x049A0x049F48 kHz.
9set clock mode 40x04B40x04C0Falls through to the common tail.
10set clock mode 50x04BC0x04C4External / S/PDIF-slaved: halves CPTRXCNF4 to 0x01 and re-enables CPTEN.
11EEPROM self-test0x04C40x04C8Sets mode 3, writes chip reg 4 = 0x41, then reads EEPROM byte 0x1F, inverts it, writes it back, re-reads and compares; the result drives a panel bit.
12set clock mode 10x05110x0478Duplicate of event 6.
13invalidate boot EEPROM0x05180x0517Writes 0x00 to EEPROM address 0 — the DFU trigger. See §6.4.
14suspend / resume0x05260x0525USB suspend entry and wake.

5. Interrupts

5.1 The 8051 vector table

Only two of the six core vectors do real work. INT0 is the USB engine; Timer 0 is the panel tick. The rest jump to shared one-byte RETI stubs, which is why the region below 0x0026 looks like scattered 0x22 and 0x32 bytes.

VectorSourceRev 20 targetRev 22 target
0x0000Reset0x0A090x092A
0x0003INT0 — USB engine0x0DAC0x0DDF
0x000BTimer 0 — panel tick0x101E0x1016
0x0013INT1 — unused0x000A0x000A
0x001BTimer 1 — unused0x000E0x000E
0x0023Serial — unused0x000F0x000F

5.2 The VECINT dispatch table

The TAS1020B multiplexes every USB event onto INT0 and reports the cause in the VECINT register (0xFFB2). The INT0 handler reads it and indexes a table of 37 big-endian addresses — one per defined VECINT value, 0x00 through 0x24. Handler names below were assigned independently of this table and match it exactly, which is a strong cross-check on both.

Every entry below 0x0030 is a one-byte RET stub; the 0x1029–0x103E band at the top of the code region is a run of such stubs, one per unused source.

6. USB

6.1 SETUP dispatch

On a SETUP interrupt the handler clears any stall on both halves of endpoint 0, sets the data-toggle bits, arms the buffers, then branches on bmRequestType read from XDATA 0xFF28. The compiler emitted this as a chain of adds against the accumulator rather than compares, which is why it reads oddly:

A = bmRequestType
A += 0xDE   ; == A - 0x22
JZ  class_out_endpoint      ; 0x22  class, host->device, endpoint
A += 0x81   ; == A - 0xA1
JZ  get_input_source        ; 0xA1  class, device->host, interface
DEC A       ; == A - 0xA2
JZ  get_sample_freq         ; 0xA2  class, device->host, endpoint
A += 0x81   ; == A - 0x21
JZ  class_out_interface     ; 0x21  class, host->device, interface
LJMP standard_request_dispatch

The four class paths cover exactly two audio controls — the input selector and the sampling-frequency endpoint control — plus the vendor trigger described in §6.4. Everything else falls through to the standard-request dispatcher.

The EP0 buffers sit at XDATA 0xFA10 (OUT) and 0xFA18 (IN), 8 bytes each, consistent with the declared bMaxPacketSize0 = 8. IRAM 0x1B:0x1C holds a working pointer into them.

6.2 Standard requests — where the revisions diverge

This is the largest structural difference between the two firmwares. Rev 20 calls the Keil ?C?CASE helper, which walks a table of {addr_hi, addr_lo, key} records comparing keys until it finds a match or hits a 00 00 terminator followed by a default address. Rev 22 drops the helper entirely and uses a dense table of LJMPs indexed by bRequest × 3 — no search, no library call, and reserved request codes get explicit stall entries instead of falling off the end.

6.3 Descriptors — and the block that is never served

The 402-byte descriptor block is byte-identical between the two revisions except for a single byte: bcdDevice at block offset +0x00C, 0x0020 in Rev 20 and 0x0022 in Rev 22.

The stock Mbox never exposes its own audio-class descriptors

Both images contain a complete, well-formed USB Audio Class configuration at block offset +0x012: an AudioControl interface with input terminals for analog and S/PDIF, a selector unit, output terminals, two AudioStreaming interfaces with 24-bit 44.1/48 kHz format descriptors, and both isochronous endpoints. It is dead data.

std_get_descriptor contains exactly five CODE pointer loads, and none of them is +0x012. GET_DESCRIPTOR(CONFIGURATION) always returns block offset +0x0DA — a 54-byte vendor-class configuration: two interfaces, bInterfaceClass = 0xFF, no endpoints on alt 0, and both iso endpoints on interface 1 alternate setting 1. Only descriptor index 0 is handled; any other index stalls. Verified by scanning both images for every pointer-load instruction pair.

This is why the device needs a proprietary driver, why Linux carries a hardcoded quirk table for 0x0DBA:0x1000 rather than parsing descriptors, and why the endpoints only appear on interface 1 alt 1. The UAC descriptors were compiled in and then not wired up.

6.4 The DFU trigger

Fully traced, and identical in both revisions:

bmRequestType 0x21, bRequest 0x00      (class, host->device, interface)
  -> setup handler reads XDATA 0xFF29 (bRequest), sees zero
  -> MOV 0x0A,#0x0D                     queue event 13
  -> event 13 handler:
       i2c_eeprom_write(addr 0x0000, value 0x00)
       OEPDCNTX0 = 0                    acknowledge on EP0

Writing zero to EEPROM byte 0 destroys the boot signature the mask ROM checks. The device keeps running the already-loaded image, so nothing appears to happen; on the next power cycle the boot ROM fails validation and stays in its own DFU mode. Any bRequest other than 0 on the same request type is treated as a class SET_CUR and tagged for an OUT data stage instead.

7. The audio path

7.1 Clock modes

All rate selection funnels through one function (Rev 20 0x0728, Rev 22 0x070F) taking a mode number in R7. The mode is stored in IRAM 0x08, and the class GET_CUR handler reads that same byte to answer host queries about the current sampling frequency — which pins the mapping without any guesswork:

ModeReported rateACG1FRQ2/1/0What it does
10 Hz (idle)—ACGCTL = 0x0D; no sample clock.
244 100 Hz6A 4B 20Both synthesizers to the 44.1 family.
348 000 Hz61 A8 0FBoth synthesizers to the 48 k family. This is what both SET_INTERFACE paths select.
4——Falls straight through to the common tail.
5external—Drops CPTEN, sets CPTRXCNF4 = 0x01 (÷2 instead of ÷4), restores CPTEN — the S/PDIF-slaved receive mode.

The common tail then writes the queued external-chip register pair, sets ACGCTL |= 0xC0 (both MCLKO outputs enabled), zeroes all four endpoint byte-count registers, re-enables both isochronous endpoint configurations at 0xC5, commits the panel shift register and spins a settling delay.

The 0xC5 endpoint configuration byte. IEPCNF1/OEPCNF2 = 0xC5 is ENABLE | ISO | BPS 5, i.e. 6 bytes per sample — two channels of 24-bit audio. It matches DMATSH = 0x80 (3 bytes per time slot) and DMATSL = 0x03 (time slots 0 and 1) on both DMA channels.

7.2 Byte order

CPTCNF3 and CPTRXCNF3 share a layout whose bit 2 is BYOR: when set, the byte order of each audio sample is reversed as it moves between the codec port and the endpoint buffer. Boot initialisation writes 0xAC (BYOR set) to both. Event 1 then rewrites CPTCNF3 by direction — 0xAC when capture is active, 0xA8 when playback is — because the DMA moves bytes the opposite way on each path, so opposite BYOR values produce the same order on the wire. Stock is big-endian in both directions, which is what Linux declares.

8. Front panel

There is no latch peripheral — both chains are driven by software loops that rotate a byte left and test bit 0 after the rotate, so bits go out most significant first. Chain A carries 8 bits from IRAM 0x22; chain B carries 16 bits, IRAM 0x23 followed by IRAM 0x25.

ChainDataClockLatchSourceRoutine (Rev 20 / Rev 22)
AP1.7P1.5P1.6IRAM 0x220x0F0C / 0x0EFC
BP1.0P1.2P1.1IRAM 0x23 then 0x250x0E62 / 0x0E56

8.1 The source-select state machine

Each of the two input channels has a three-position selector cycled by a front-panel button. Channel A occupies IRAM 0x22 bits 2:0 and channel B bits 5:3. The cycle is driven by two hidden state bits per channel, and it runs in a fixed order:

state (0,0) --press--> emit 0b101 (0x05)   state becomes (1,1)
state (1,1) --press--> emit 0b011 (0x03)   state becomes (1,0)
state (1,0) --press--> emit 0b110 (0x06)   state becomes (0,0)

So the ring is 0x05 → 0x03 → 0x06 → 0x05, which against the panel legend makes 0x05 = Mic, 0x03 = Line, 0x06 = Inst. Channel B is the same machine on bits 0x29/0x2B driving output bits 3, 4 and 5.

Direction matters

Any reimplementation that cycles 0x05 → 0x06 → 0x03 traverses the same three values in the opposite order. The selector still works but the button steps backwards through the panel legend.

Both channel routines end with the same tail, which derives IRAM 0x22 bit 6 from two other flags: it is set when bit 0x2C is clear, and forced clear when bit 0x2D is set. Bit 0x2C is the analog-versus-S/PDIF selection — the very bit the class GET_CUR handler reports as selector-unit pin 1 or 2. Bit 0x22.6 is therefore a derived output, not an independent control.

The third button, on P3.5, calls a nine-byte routine that is a pure toggle of bit 0x1E — IRAM 0x23 bit 6 — which rides out on chain B and additionally changes how chain A finishes its latch sequence. It is a firmware-owned latched output with no other dependency.

9. Rev 20 versus Rev 22

The two images are the same program. Function-level structural matching pairs 94 of them; the differences are concentrated in three places.

9.1 The headline: a playback DMA watchdog

Rev 20 points VECINT 0x14 (SOF) at a one-byte RET. Rev 22 points it at a 70-byte handler that runs every USB frame:

It reads the 16-bit playback DMA byte counter, and if it has moved since the last frame, divides it by 6 — the size of one stereo 24-bit sample frame — using a 16-bit division routine that exists in Rev 22 solely to support this check. If the remainder is non-zero the playback stream has slipped out of sample alignment, and the handler stops the DMA channel, clears both ping-pong buffer counts, re-enables the OUT endpoint and restarts the channel.

A misalignment of anything other than a whole number of 6-byte frames permanently rotates the channel and byte order of the playback stream. Rev 20 has no way to detect or recover from it; Rev 22 checks once per millisecond and resynchronises. This is the most plausible mechanical explanation for Rev 20 being the revision that misbehaves on playback.

Note also what Rev 22 did not need to change: Rev 20 already unmasks SOF (USBIMSK = 0xFF when interface 1 goes to a non-zero alt setting), so the interrupt was already firing — into a stub. The fix is one table entry plus a handler.

9.2 Restructuring, same behaviour

AreaRev 20Rev 22
Standard-request dispatchKeil ?C?CASE helper + 37-byte searched key table39-byte dense LJMP table indexed by bRequest
SETUP handlingone 47-byte dispatcher calling four separate class handlersa single 218-byte handler with all four inlined
Event index mathADD A,R0 twiceMUL AB with B = 3
External chip writesregister/value staged through IRAM 0x2C–0x2F, then a thunkregister and value passed directly in R7/R5
Delay loopscounted in IRAM byte 0x2Ecounted in R7
Panel scan resultreturned in R7left in IRAM 0x07 for the caller
C51 initialiser table13 records12 records — Rev 20 additionally initialises IRAM 0x08 to 3

9.3 What is byte-identical

The descriptor block differs by one byte (bcdDevice). The VECINT table differs only in relocated handler addresses plus the SOF entry. The clock-mode values, the endpoint configuration bytes, the DMA transfer sizes, the shift-register bit order, the source-select state machine and the DFU trigger are all unchanged.

10. Internal RAM map

Addresses used as bit operands are shown with their owning byte. Register banks occupy 0x00–0x1F; the bit-addressable region is 0x20–0x2F.

IRAMMeaning
0x08current clock mode (1,2,3,5) — read back by the class GET_CUR sampling-frequency handler
0x0Apending event code, 1–14; zero means idle
0x0Dpending class-request tag (1 = endpoint, 2 = interface, 5 = deferred SET_ADDRESS)
0x0Edeferred USB device address, written to USBFADR after the status stage
0x19:0x1ACODE pointer for descriptor reads (MOVC)
0x1B:0x1CXDATA pointer into the EP0 buffers
0x20previous Port 3 sample, for edge detection
0x21bit 0 = interface 1 alt, bit 1 = interface 2 alt, bit 2 = (vestigial, never set), bit 6 = configured
0x22shift-register chain A: bits 2:0 channel A source, bits 5:3 channel B source, bit 6 derived, bit 7 mute/idle
0x23shift-register chain B low byte; bit 6 toggled by the P3.5 button
0x24bit 0 = Timer 0 tick flag
0x25shift-register chain B high byte; bit 4 = S/PDIF selected, bit 5 = forces the derived bit clear
0x27suspend/resume edge state
0x28–0x2Bhidden selector state bits, two per channel
0x2C–0x2Fstaged external-chip register/value pairs and delay counters (Rev 20 only)
0x31:0x32queued chip register/value applied by the clock-mode tail

13. Provenance and confidence

Structure — instruction boundaries, control flow, byte classification, table contents, descriptor fields — is derived mechanically and is exact. Where a name expresses an interpretation of intent rather than a decoded fact, that interpretation rests on one of: a register's documented function in the TAS1020B datasheet, a matching constant in TI's USB-audio reference sources, agreement between the two independent revisions, or a value the firmware itself reports back over USB.

The strongest internal cross-check available: the VECINT handler names were assigned without reference to the dispatch table, and all 37 entries line up — SETUP to the SETUP handler, SUSR to the suspend handler, IEP0 to the EP0-IN handler, every unused source to a RET stub. Similarly, the clock-mode numbering is confirmed by the firmware answering 44100 and 48000 to the host from the same IRAM byte the mode setter writes.

Generated by tools/build_fw_doc.py directly from rev20_firmware_code.bin, rev22_firmware_code.bin and the two Ghidra recursive-traversal listings. Every table, count and listing on this page is produced at build time; none is transcribed by hand.

14. Decompiled C

Both images were also run through a decompiler. Coverage is complete — every function in both images has a C body — and the SFR reads and writes resolve to named registers, which makes the audio and USB paths far easier to follow than the assembly. Treat it as a readable second view, not as ground truth: the assembly and the raw bytes remain authoritative.

ImageFunctionsWith C bodyDecompiler failuresLines of C
Rev 2012612623166
Rev 2211211202869

The only two decompiler failures, and why they are not unknowns

Both are in Rev 20 and both are the same construct: the Keil ?C?CASE helper at 0x0F70 pops its own return address off the stack and uses it as a pointer to an inline key table. The decompiler models the call as never returning and flags the 37 table bytes at 0x011F as bad instruction data. That table is fully decoded in §6.2 — eleven {addr, bRequest} records plus a default. Rev 22 has zero failures because it replaced the helper with a dense LJMP table.

14.1 Side by side

Three functions where the C view earns its keep.

Rev 22 sof_int_handler

Rev 22's playback watchdog. The % 6 is the whole point and it is invisible in the assembly, where it is a call to a 16-bit division helper.

/* sof_int_handler — SOF_INT: audio-OUT DMA 6-byte alignment resync */

void sof_int_handler(void)

{
  char cVar1;
  char cVar2;
  byte bVar3;
  
  cVar1 = '\0';
  bVar3 = DMABCNT0L ^ BANK3_R4;
  if (bVar3 == 0) {
    bVar3 = DMABCNT0H ^ BANK3_R3;
  }
  if (bVar3 != 0) {
    BANK3_R3 = DMABCNT0H;
    BANK3_R4 = DMABCNT0L;
    cVar2 = '\x06';
    udiv16();
    if (cVar2 != '\0' || cVar1 != '\0') {
      OEPDCNTX2 = 0;
      OEPDCNTY2 = 0;
      OEPCNF2 = 0xc5;
      DMACTL0 = DMACTL0 & 0x7f | 0x80;
    }
  }
  return;
}
